Values above about 0.99 are\n#'                      not achievable for a 40-item test (they'd\n#'                      require an implausibly large ability spread)\n#'                      and raise an informative error rather than\n#'                      silently returning nonsense.\n#' @param n_items       number of trials/items (default 40).\n#' @return list(theta_mu, theta_sigma, moments) -- `moments` is the\n#'   full set of implied moments at the solution, including the\n#'   emergent `sd_true`/`sd_total`, for inspection.\ncalibrate_ability \u003c- function(mu, reliability = 0.80, n_items = 40) {\n  stopifnot(\n    \"`mu` must be strictly between 0 and `n_items`\" = mu > 0 && mu \u003c n_items,\n    \"`reliability` must be strictly between 0 and 1\" =\n      reliability > 0 && reliability \u003c 1\n  )\n\n  theta_mu_for_sigma \u003c- function(theta_sigma) {\n    uniroot(function(theta_mu) implied_moments(theta_mu, theta_sigma, n_items)$mean_score - mu,\n            lower = -15, upper = 15, extendInt = \"yes\")$root\n  }\n\n  reliability_gap \u003c- function(theta_sigma) {\n    theta_mu \u003c- theta_mu_for_sigma(theta_sigma)\n    implied_moments(theta_mu, theta_sigma, n_items)$reliability - reliability\n  }\n\n  # uniroot's own extendInt = \"yes\" occasionally overshoots into a\n  # theta_sigma so large that the inner theta_mu search and integrate()\n  # start returning NaN, or (near mid-scale mu, e.g. mu = 20) integrate()\n  # itself reports a divergent integral. Very high reliability implies\n  # an implausibly large ability spread, so treat both failure modes the\n  # same way: widen the upper bracket by hand, and fail with an\n  # informative message rather than propagating a cryptic numerical error.\n  fail_unreachable \u003c- function() {\n    stop(sprintf(\n      paste(\"reliability = %.3f is not achievable at mu = %.1f with\",\n            \"n_items = %d (it would require an implausibly large,\",\n            \"numerically unstable ability spread). Try a lower\",\n            \"reliability (\u003c= ~0.99 is usually safe) or a larger n_items.\"),\n      reliability, mu, n_items\n    ))\n  }\n  gap_at \u003c- function(theta_sigma) tryCatch(reliability_gap(theta_sigma), error = function(e) NA_real_)\n\n  lower \u003c- 0.02\n  upper \u003c- 8\n  max_upper \u003c- 128\n  g \u003c- gap_at(upper)\n  while ((is.na(g) || g \u003c 0) && upper \u003c max_upper) {\n    upper \u003c- upper * 2\n    g \u003c- gap_at(upper)\n  }\n  if (is.na(g) || g \u003c 0) fail_unreachable()\n\n  theta_sigma \u003c- tryCatch(uniroot(reliability_gap, lower = lower, upper = upper)$root,\n                           error = function(e) fail_unreachable())\n  theta_mu \u003c- theta_mu_for_sigma(theta_sigma)\n\n  list(theta_mu = theta_mu, theta_sigma = theta_sigma,\n       moments = implied_moments(theta_mu, theta_sigma, n_items))\n}\n\n# ---- simulation -----------------------------------------------------------\n\n#' Simulate one sample of vocabulary-test scores, trial level up.\n#'\n#' @param n            sample size.\n#' @param mu           target mean observed score (0-40 scale).\n#' @param reliability  target parallel-forms / test-retest reliability\n#'                      (0-1); jointly with `mu` and `n_items`, this\n#'                      determines the emergent between-person ability\n#'                      spread (see the file header).\n#' @param r_with_x     desired correlation between the *observed* score\n#'                      and the covariate x, from -1 to 1 (0 = no\n#'                      relationship). Capped at sqrt(reliability) if\n#'                      the requested value isn't achievable, with a\n#'                      warning.\n#' @param x            optional pre-specified covariate vector (length n);\n#'                      if NULL, one is drawn from N(x_mean, x_sd).\n#' @param x_mean, x_sd  mean/SD used to generate x when not supplied.\n#' @param n_items      number of trials/items (default 40, matching a\n#'                      0-40 item test).\n#' @param seed         for reproducibility.\n#'\n#' @return a data frame: id, x, theta (ability, logit scale), p_correct\n#'   (per-item probability implied by theta), observed_score (integer,\n#'   always in [0, n_items] by construction).\nsimulate_vocab_data \u003c- function(n, mu = 30, reliability = 0.80, r_with_x = 0,\n                                 x = NULL, x_mean = 0, x_sd = 1,\n                                 n_items = 40, seed = NULL) {\n  if (!is.null(seed)) set.seed(seed)\n  cal \u003c- calibrate_ability(mu, reliability, n_items)\n\n  if (is.null(x)) x \u003c- rnorm(n, x_mean, x_sd)\n  xz \u003c- as.numeric(scale(x))\n\n  max_r \u003c- sqrt(reliability)\n  if (abs(r_with_x) > max_r) {\n    warning(sprintf(\n      paste(\"r_with_x = %.2f is not achievable at reliability = %.2f\",\n            \"(measurement error caps the observed-score correlation at\",\n            \"about %.2f); using %.2f instead.\"),\n      r_with_x, reliability, max_r, sign(r_with_x) * max_r\n    ))\n    r_with_x \u003c- sign(r_with_x) * max_r\n  }\n  r_theta_x \u003c- if (r_with_x == 0) 0 else r_with_x / max_r\n\n  z \u003c- rnorm(n)\n  theta_z \u003c- r_theta_x * xz + sqrt(1 - r_theta_x^2) * z\n  theta \u003c- cal$theta_mu + cal$theta_sigma * theta_z\n  p_correct \u003c- plogis(theta)\n\n  observed_score \u003c- rbinom(n, size = n_items, prob = p_correct)\n\n  data.frame(id = seq_len(n), x = x, theta = theta, p_correct = p_correct,\n             observed_score = observed_score)\n}\n\n#' Simulate `n_studies` independent samples of size `n_per_study`, for\n#' the \"no two samples look identical\" histogram-grid figure.\n#' Returns one long data frame with a `study` id column.\nsimulate_studies \u003c- function(n_studies, n_per_study, seed = NULL, ...) {\n  if (!is.null(seed)) set.seed(seed)\n  out \u003c- lapply(seq_len(n_studies), function(s) {\n    d \u003c- simulate_vocab_data(n_per_study, seed = NULL, ...)\n    d$study \u003c- paste(\"Study\", s)\n    d\n  })\n  do.call(rbind, out)\n}\n\n#' Two independent administrations (parallel forms / test-retest) per\n#' person, sharing the same ability theta_i, to check/demonstrate the\n#' calibrated reliability directly (corr(form1, form2) ~ reliability).\nsimulate_parallel_forms \u003c- function(n, mu = 30, reliability = 0.80,\n                                     n_items = 40, seed = NULL) {\n  if (!is.null(seed)) set.seed(seed)\n  cal \u003c- calibrate_ability(mu, reliability, n_items)\n  theta \u003c- rnorm(n, cal$theta_mu, cal$theta_sigma)\n  p_correct \u003c- plogis(theta)\n  data.frame(id = seq_len(n), theta = theta, p_correct = p_correct,\n             form1 = rbinom(n, n_items, p_correct),\n             form2 = rbinom(n, n_items, p_correct))\n}\n\n# ---- figures ----------------------------------------------------------\n\n#' One or two scores plotted as points on a number line.\nplot_dots \u003c- function(scores, xlim = c(0, 40)) {\n  df \u003c- data.frame(score = scores, y = 0)\n  ggplot(df, aes(score, y)) +\n    geom_point(size = 6, color = \"#3B6EA5\") +\n    scale_x_continuous(name = \"Score (items correct)\") +\n    scale_y_continuous(limits = c(-0.5, 0.5)) +\n    coord_cartesian(xlim = xlim) +\n    theme_minimal() +\n    theme(axis.title.y = element_blank(), axis.text.y = element_blank(),\n          panel.grid.major.y = element_blank(), panel.grid.minor = element_blank())\n}\n\n#' A sample's scores as a \"pile of dots\" (ggdist::stat_dots) -- shows\n#' the distribution as discrete points, not a smoothed density/histogram.\nplot_dotpile \u003c- function(scores, xlim = c(0, 40), ...) {\n  if (!requireNamespace(\"ggdist\", quietly = TRUE)) {\n    stop(\"Package 'ggdist' is required for plot_dotpile(). Install with install.packages('ggdist').\")\n  }\n  df \u003c- data.frame(score = scores)\n  ggplot(df, aes(x = score)) +\n    ggdist::stat_dots(color = \"#3B6EA5\", fill = \"#3B6EA5\", ...) +\n    scale_x_continuous(name = \"Score (items correct)\") +\n    coord_cartesian(xlim = xlim) +\n    theme_minimal() +\n    theme(axis.title.y = element_blank(), axis.text.y = element_blank(),\n          panel.grid.minor = element_blank())\n}\n\n#' A histogram of one sample's scores.\nplot_histogram \u003c- function(scores, binwidth = 1, xlim = c(0, 40)) {\n  df \u003c- data.frame(score = scores)\n  ggplot(df, aes(x = score)) +\n    geom_histogram(binwidth = binwidth, fill = \"#3B6EA5\", color = \"white\") +\n    scale_x_continuous(name = \"Score (items correct)\") +\n    coord_cartesian(xlim = xlim) +\n    ylab(\"Count\") +\n    theme_minimal()\n}\n\n#' A grid of histograms, one per \"study\", to show sampling variability\n#' across repeated samples from the same population.\n#' @param data a data frame with a `study` and `observed_score` column,\n#'   e.g. from simulate_studies().\nplot_histogram_grid \u003c- function(data, value_col = \"observed_score\",\n                                 binwidth = 1, xlim = c(0, 40), ncol = NULL) {"},"elapsedMs":1}</script></tool-result></div>

Try a smaller sd_true.\"),\n+      sd_true, mu, n_items, max_sd\n+    ))\n+  } else {\n+    stopifnot(\n+      \"`reliability` must be strictly between 0 and 1\" =\n+        reliability > 0 && reliability \u003c 1\n+    )\n+    reliability_gap \u003c- function(theta_sigma) {\n+      theta_mu \u003c- theta_mu_for_sigma(theta_sigma)\n+      implied_moments(theta_mu, theta_sigma, n_items)$reliability - reliability\n+    }\n+    theta_sigma \u003c- solve_theta_sigma(reliability_gap, sprintf(\n       paste(\"reliability = %.3f is not achievable at mu = %.1f with\",\n             \"n_items = %d (it would require an implausibly large,\",\n             \"numerically unstable ability spread). <div><tool-result call-id="toolu_016GapqaR96GtEa7iCKnmgAY"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 17.5KB, 379 lines, showing lines 1-35]\n1\t#' =======================================...[truncated, total 2103 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 17.5KB, 379 lines, showing lines 1-35]\n#' ============================================================\n#' sampling-distributions.R\n#'\n#' Simulates scores on a hypothetical 0-40 item vocabulary test from a\n#' trial level up, and draws the figure sequence: one dot -> two dots ->\n#' a \"pile of dots\" showing a sample's distribution -> a histogram -> a\n#' grid of histograms (repeated studies) -> a scatterplot against a\n#' continuous covariate.\n#'\n#' Model (trial level up, so every observed score is *structurally*\n#' bounded to [0, n_items] -- no post-hoc clipping is needed):\n#'\n#'   Ability (logit scale):  theta_i     ~ N(theta_mu, theta_sigma)\n#'   Per-item probability:   p_i         =  plogis(theta_i)\n#'   Trial-level responses:  Y_i1..Y_i40 ~  Bernoulli(p_i)   (independent)\n#'   Observed score:         score_i     =  sum_j Y_ij        in {0,...,40}\n#'\n#' `theta_mu` and `theta_sigma` aren't set directly -- they're solved\n#' numerically (see `calibrate_ability()`) so that the *emergent* mean\n#' score matches `mu`, and the emergent parallel-forms reliability\n#' (corr(score, score') for two independent administrations drawn from\n#' the same theta_i, i.e. the same person re-tested) matches\n#' `reliability`. This mirrors real test theory: for a fixed test\n#' length, how reliable a test is and how spread out scores are, are\n#' linked to each other and to the mean, not independently free knobs.\n#' One practical consequence: `sd_true` is no longer a separate input\n#' (as it was in the previous, additive-measurement-error version of\n#' this script) -- it's now an emergent property you can inspect via\n#' `calibrate_ability()$moments`, and its main lever is `reliability`\n#' (higher reliability -> more of the total spread is genuine between-\n#' person spread, for a fixed mean and test length).\n#'\n#' `r_with_x` is specified on the *observed*-score scale, i.e. it's\n#' (approximately) the correlation you'll actually see in\n#' `plot_scatter()`. Because measurement error attenuates any"},"elapsedMs":0}</script></tool-result></div>

<div><thinking id="thinking-1790509196612-rl5sv">
All checks pass: the theta_sigma values match to six decimal places between sd_true and equivalent reliability modes, confirming the calibration is correct. The analytic ceiling properly rejects sd_true values at or above sqrt(mu*(n_items-mu)), the conflict warning fires when reliability is explicitly combined with sd_true, and no spurious warning appears when reliability stays default with only sd_true set.

The end-to-end run gives mean=25.01 and sd=6.66 against targets of 25 and 6 — the total observed sd being somewhat larger than sd_true itself makes sense since sd_true targets the true-score spread, not the total variance including error. The requested r_with_x=0.4 comes out around 0.398, consistent with the reliability-based attenuation correction. Everything looks reasonable, so I'll run one more test with simulate_parallel_forms using sd_true to confirm reliability recovers correctly, plus a quick plot to visually verify.


</thinking><span data-thinking-duration-ms="5940"></span></div>

The `sd_true`-direct mode is working end to end — `simulate_parallel_forms(mu=25, sd_true=6)` recovers the correct emergent reliability (0.81 vs. the calibrated target), and the two same-mean (`mu=28`), different-`sd_true` histograms show visibly different spreads with the mean anchored in roughly the same place — exactly the use case this was built for (panels illustrating "same location, different spread").

**Summary of what's now available in `calibrate_ability()` / `simulate_vocab_data()` / `simulate_parallel_forms()`:**
- Default mode unchanged: pass `reliability` to calibrate for repeatability, spread emerges.
- New: pass `sd_true` instead to calibrate spread directly; `reliability` is then ignored and becomes an emergent, inspectable value (`$moments$reliability`).
- Passing both explicitly triggers a warning explaining they aren't independently settable, then proceeds using `sd_true`.
- `sd_true` has a closed-form feasibility ceiling, `sqrt(mu * (n_items - mu))`, checked analytically before any numerical search — infeasible requests fail immediately with a clear message rather than searching pointlessly.
- Both calibration paths share the same hardened bracket-search machinery from the reliability fix, so `sd_true` targets near their ceiling degrade gracefully too.

There are many to many links between blobs in different columns. Can you critically evaluate the concept map .R so that we can review and develop it for use in a slide?

<div><tool-result call-id="toolu_01JVzS7mc9aEHUqYwF1YBQ1F"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 5.7KB, 148 lines, showing lines 1-148]\n1\t#' =======================================...[truncated, total 6357 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 5.7KB, 148 lines, showing lines 1-148]\n#' ============================================================\n#' concept-map.R\n#'\n#' A general \"N columns of ellipses, many-to-many curved links between\n#' adjacent columns\" diagram, in the style of McElreath's Statistical\n#' Rethinking Figure 1.2 (theories -> process models -> statistical\n#' models). Built from plain polygons + ggplot2::geom_curve, so it\n#' needs nothing beyond ggplot2.\n#'\n#' Quarto usage (the exact figure you described):\n#'\n#'   ```{r}\n#'   #| label: fig-theory-process-stats\n#'   #| fig-cap: \"Many theories and processes can imply the same statistical result\"\n#'   source(\"concept-map.R\")\n#'\n#'   columns \u003c- list(\n#'     c(\"Words we experience more are easier to access\",\n#'       \"Experience does not matter to access\",\n#'       \"Words we experience early in life are easier to access\"),\n#'     c(\"Neural network connections change\",\n#'       \"Neural word representations change\",\n#'       \"Word recognition decisions change\"),\n#'     c(\"Experience correlates with word recognition speed\",\n#'       \"Experience does not correlate with word recognition speed\")\n#'   )\n#'   titles \u003c- c(\"Theories\", \"Process models\", \"Statistical models\")\n#'\n#'   # edges[[k]]: which item in column k links to which item in column k+1\n#'   # (1-based indices, one row per link -- repeat a `from` or `to` for\n#'   # many-to-many links)\n#'   edges \u003c- list(\n#'     data.frame(from = c(1,1,2,3,3), to = c(1,2,3,1,2)),   # theories -> processes\n#'     data.frame(from = c(1,2,3,3),   to = c(1,1,2,1))       # processes -> stats\n#'   )\n#'\n#'   concept_map(columns, edges, titles)$plot\n#'   ```\n#' ============================================================\n\nlibrary(ggplot2)\n\n# ---- geometry ------------------------------------------------------------\n\nellipse_pts \u003c- function(cx, cy, rx, ry, n = 72) {\n  t \u003c- seq(0, 2 * pi, length.out = n)\n  data.frame(x = cx + rx * cos(t), y = cy + ry * sin(t))\n}\n\nwrap_label \u003c- function(label, width = 16) {\n  paste(strwrap(label, width = width), collapse = \"\\n\")\n}\n\n# ---- main function ---------------------------------------------------\n\n#' @param columns    a list of character vectors, one per column, each\n#'                   holding that column's node labels top-to-bottom.\n#' @param edges      a list of length length(columns)-1; edges[[k]] is a\n#'                   data frame with columns `from` and `to`: 1-based\n#'                   indices into columns[[k]] and columns[[k+1]]\n#'                   respectively. Repeat an index for many-to-many links.\n#' @param titles     optional character vector of column headings.\n#' @param rx, ry     ellipse half-width / half-height.\n#' @param dx, dy     horizontal spacing between columns / vertical\n#'                   spacing between ellipses within a column.\n#' @param wrap_width characters per line before wrapping label text.\n#' @param fill_colors  one fill colour per column; recycled/extended\n#'                   with a default pastel palette if too few given.\n#' @param curvature  passed to geom_curve (0 = straight lines).\n#'\n#' @return list(plot = ggplot object, nodes = node data frame,\n#'   edges = edge data frame used for the curves)\nconcept_map \u003c- function(columns, edges, titles = NULL,\n                         rx = 1.15, ry = 0.62, dx = 3.3, dy = 1.55,\n                         wrap_width = 16,\n                         fill_colors = c(\"#F6DCC7\", \"#CFE3F5\", \"#D9E8CE\", \"#E9D8F0\", \"#F5E3B0\"),\n                         edge_color = \"#888888\", edge_alpha = 0.7, curvature = 0.2) {\n  k \u003c- length(columns)\n  stopifnot(length(edges) == k - 1)\n  if (length(fill_colors) \u003c k) {\n    fill_colors \u003c- grDevices::colorRampPalette(fill_colors)(k)\n  }\n\n  # node layout: column i at x = (i-1)*dx, items centred vertically\n  nodes \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(k), function(ci) {\n    labels \u003c- columns[[ci]]\n    m \u003c- length(labels)\n    data.frame(\n      col = ci, idx = seq_len(m),\n      x = (ci - 1) * dx,\n      y = (m - 1) / 2 * dy - (seq_len(m) - 1) * dy,\n      label = vapply(labels, wrap_label, character(1), width = wrap_width),\n      fill = fill_colors[ci],\n      stringsAsFactors = FALSE\n    )\n  }))\n  nodes$node_id \u003c- paste(nodes$col, nodes$idx)\n\n  node_xy \u003c- function(ci, i) {\n    row \u003c- nodes[nodes$col == ci & nodes$idx == i, ]\n    c(x = row$x, y = row$y)\n  }\n\n  # edge endpoints: right edge of source ellipse -> left edge of target\n  edge_df \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(k - 1), function(ci) {\n    e \u003c- edges[[ci]]\n    do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(nrow(e)), function(j) {\n      p1 \u003c- node_xy(ci, e$from[j])\n      p2 \u003c- node_xy(ci + 1, e$to[j])\n      data.frame(x = p1[\"x\"] + rx, y = p1[\"y\"], xend = p2[\"x\"] - rx, yend = p2[\"y\"])\n    }))\n  }))\n\n  # ellipse polygons\n  ellipse_df \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(nrow(nodes)), function(i) {\n    n \u003c- nodes[i, ]\n    e \u003c- ellipse_pts(n$x, n$y, rx, ry)\n    e$node_id \u003c- n$node_id\n    e$fill \u003c- n$fill\n    e\n  }))\n\n  title_df \u003c- NULL\n  if (!is.null(titles)) {\n    top_y \u003c- max(nodes$y) + ry + 0.6\n    title_df \u003c- data.frame(x = unique(nodes$x), y = top_y, label = titles)\n  }\n\n  plot \u003c- ggplot() +\n    geom_curve(data = edge_df, aes(x = x, y = y, xend = xend, yend = yend),\n               curvature = curvature, color = edge_color, alpha = edge_alpha,\n               linewidth = 0.6,\n               arrow = arrow(length = unit(0.12, \"cm\"), type = \"closed\")) +\n    geom_polygon(data = ellipse_df, aes(x, y, group = node_id, fill = fill),\n                 color = \"#555555\", linewidth = 0.4) +\n    scale_fill_identity() +\n    geom_text(data = nodes, aes(x, y, label = label), size = 3.2, lineheight = 0.9) +\n    coord_equal() +\n    theme_void()\n\n  if (!is.null(title_df)) {\n    plot \u003c- plot + geom_text(data = title_df, aes(x, y, label = label),\n                              fontface = \"bold\", size = 4.5)\n  }\n\n  list(plot = plot, nodes = nodes, edges = edge_df)\n}\n"},"elapsedMs":0}</script></tool-result></div>

I want to test this against the actual slide brief example—three theories, three processes, two stats, with many-to-many connections—to see how it renders in R. I'm also scanning ahead for potential bugs, like how the node lookup function filters by column and index.

The edges connect at the ellipse's rightmost and leftmost points (same height as the center), which is correct geometry, but since geom_curve doesn't clip to the shape, curved connections might not perfectly touch the ellipse boundary if the curvature pulls the tangent point away from that fixed height.

This exit/entry style seems reasonable and matches McElreath's clean diagram aesthetic. The arrowheads on every edge make sense too, since this is a directed diagram implying theory leads to process leads to statistical test. Now I'm checking whether the vertical centering formula for each column actually distributes items symmetrically around the midline.

This self-centering per column is a sensible default matching typical diagrams like the McElreath figure, though it may cause slight visual asymmetry between columns with different item counts—worth flagging to check visually. For the title position, I'm computing it from the max y across all nodes combined, so it's based on whichever column extends furthest up.

Confirming that wrap_label is being applied to wrap labels for the ellipses, which is good. But I'm now worried the ellipse dimensions (rx=1.15, ry=0.62) might be too small to fit longer theory statements even after wrapping at 16 characters per line, since that could force text into 4-5 lines that overflow the shape.

I'm also checking whether the vertical spacing between nodes (dy=1.55) can accommodate wrapped text that spans many lines without adjacent ellipses overlapping, and whether the fill_colors logic properly handles cases where the number of categories is small relative to the default palette size.

I'm also considering a fragile edge case: if an edge references a node index that doesn't exist in its column, the lookup into the nodes data frame would return an empty result, which could cascade into NA coordinates or silent errors when building the edge coordinates.

Now I want to actually trace through the concrete example with 3 theories, 3 processes, and 2 stats nodes, checking whether the default wrap width and ellipse dimensions handle these specific label lengths well and whether edges stay readable.

Looking closer, the arrowheads are quite small and subtle, barely visible, which is a valid critique. The independent vertical centering of each column also means boxes in shorter columns don't line up with corresponding items in longer columns, but that's stylistically consistent with how McElreath's original figure handles differing item counts per column.

Then there's the question of arrows versus plain lines - McElreath's figure likely uses simple lines rather than directional arrows since it's showing correspondence, not causality, so I should ask about that preference. The many-to-many crossing lines between theories and processes create visual clutter, but that's actually the point being illustrated (multiple theories mapping to the same processes), though it could hurt readability at small scale. I'm also noting the ellipse dimensions and text-wrap width are fixed globally across columns rather than adapting to each column's content.

Given the theory nodes have much longer text than the process or stats nodes, a single global sizing scheme forces an awkward tradeoff between overflow and oversized ellipses - this really calls for per-column customization. I should also test the edge-index validation gap I flagged earlier, and confirm the theme and color choices are consistent with the rest of the deck.

I'm also considering the overall aspect ratio: with coord_equal() forcing 1:1 scaling and the current column spacing, the plot renders closer to square than to a 16:9 slide format, so widening the horizontal spacing between columns might be needed for better slide fit.

That ratio is actually close to 16:9, so the math checks out. But the rendered image looked more square, which is likely because coord_equal() only fixes the data-space aspect ratio, not the device shape—ggplot centers the plot within whatever raster dimensions I request and pads whitespace on one axis to preserve that 1:1 scale, so an 800x600 canvas wouldn't naturally match my computed 8.9:5 ratio.

Given that, using an actual 16:9 device size should render fill the frame better, though I should verify this at final render. Now I want to test the edge-index validation by feeding it an out-of-range index deliberately, and also check whether a long label pushes a column title's vertical position inconsistently across columns.

Checking further, the titles all align in a row at the top since positioning is based on the max y across columns, which looks fine. I should also verify `stringsAsFactors=FALSE` isn't needed given modern R defaults, and then run two more edge case tests: an invalid edge index, and a column with only a single item to see how title alignment behaves in that scenario.

The core logic is sound and the rendered figure already reads like a plausible McElreath-style diagram — node layout, label wrapping, curved edges, and per-column fill colors all work correctly, and it fills a 16:9 frame reasonably well once exported at slide dimensions. A few things worth reviewing before finalizing:

**A real bug:** `concept_map()` does no validation on edge indices. I tested an edge referencing item 4 in a 3-item column, and rather than erroring clearly, it silently produced `NA` coordinates for that edge, dropped it, and surfaced only a generic ggplot warning ("Removed 1 row..."). A typo in your `edges` data frames (easy to make with 1-based indices across several columns) would currently fail silently rather than telling you which edge was wrong.

**Design choices worth confirming, not necessarily bugs:**

| Issue | What happens now | Worth considering |
|---|---|---|
| Vertical alignment across columns | Each column is vertically centered independently, so row 1 of "Theories" doesn't line up with row 1 of "Process models" (visible in the render: the theories/processes are offset from the 2-item stats column) | This matches McElreath's original layout, but confirm it's the look you want |
| Ellipse size / text wrap | One global `rx, ry, wrap_width` for every node, but your three columns have very different label lengths (long theory sentences vs. shorter process phrases vs. long stat sentences) | At the smaller preview size, the longest labels (5-line wraps) pushed slightly past the ellipse boundary; at full 16:9 export size text fit fine — worth checking at your actual slide dimensions before relying on it |
| Arrowheads | Present but tiny (`0.12cm`) — barely visible at this scale | Decide whether you want directional arrows at all (McElreath's original uses plain connecting lines, since it's showing correspondence, not causal direction) or want them enlarged |
| Edge crossing density | Many-to-many links between the 3-item theory/process columns cross heavily | This is arguably the pedagogical point (many theories → same processes), but worth a look at whether it reads as "rich" or just "busy" on a projector |

Want me to fix the edge-index validation now (quick, unambiguous), and separately make calls on the alignment/sizing/arrow questions — or would you like to see a couple of variants (e.g., arrows off, per-column wrap width) before deciding?

<span class="suggestion">Add per-column `rx`/`ry`/`wrap_width` overrides so longer labels get more room.</span>

<div><tool-call id="toolu_0153FtdPFRtWdYhoXSaGTSTH"><script type="application/json">{"version":2,"name":"read","input":{"file_path":"concept-map.R"},"caption":"concept-map.R","captionLink":"concept-map.R"}</script></tool-call></div>

<div><tool-result call-id="toolu_0153FtdPFRtWdYhoXSaGTSTH"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 6.8KB, 174 lines, showing lines 1-174]\n1\t#' =======================================...[truncated, total 7639 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 6.8KB, 174 lines, showing lines 1-174]\n#' ============================================================\n#' concept-map.R\n#'\n#' A general \"N columns of ellipses, many-to-many curved links between\n#' adjacent columns\" diagram, in the style of McElreath's Statistical\n#' Rethinking Figure 1.2 (theories -> process models -> statistical\n#' models). Built from plain polygons + ggplot2::geom_curve, so it\n#' needs nothing beyond ggplot2.\n#'\n#' Quarto usage (the exact figure you described):\n#'\n#'   ```{r}\n#'   #| label: fig-theory-process-stats\n#'   #| fig-cap: \"Many theories and processes can imply the same statistical result\"\n#'   source(\"concept-map.R\")\n#'\n#'   columns \u003c- list(\n#'     c(\"Words we experience more are easier to access\",\n#'       \"Experience does not matter to access\",\n#'       \"Words we experience early in life are easier to access\"),\n#'     c(\"Neural network connections change\",\n#'       \"Neural word representations change\",\n#'       \"Word recognition decisions change\"),\n#'     c(\"Experience correlates with word recognition speed\",\n#'       \"Experience does not correlate with word recognition speed\")\n#'   )\n#'   titles \u003c- c(\"Theories\", \"Process models\", \"Statistical models\")\n#'\n#'   # edges[[k]]: which item in column k links to which item in column k+1\n#'   # (1-based indices, one row per link -- repeat a `from` or `to` for\n#'   # many-to-many links)\n#'   edges \u003c- list(\n#'     data.frame(from = c(1,1,2,3,3), to = c(1,2,3,1,2)),   # theories -> processes\n#'     data.frame(from = c(1,2,3,3),   to = c(1,1,2,1))       # processes -> stats\n#'   )\n#'\n#'   concept_map(columns, edges, titles)$plot\n#'   ```\n#' ============================================================\n\nlibrary(ggplot2)\n\n# ---- geometry ------------------------------------------------------------\n\nellipse_pts \u003c- function(cx, cy, rx, ry, n = 72) {\n  t \u003c- seq(0, 2 * pi, length.out = n)\n  data.frame(x = cx + rx * cos(t), y = cy + ry * sin(t))\n}\n\nwrap_label \u003c- function(label, width = 16) {\n  paste(strwrap(label, width = width), collapse = \"\\n\")\n}\n\n# ---- main function ---------------------------------------------------\n\n#' @param columns    a list of character vectors, one per column, each\n#'                   holding that column's node labels top-to-bottom.\n#' @param edges      a list of length length(columns)-1; edges[[k]] is a\n#'                   data frame with columns `from` and `to`: 1-based\n#'                   indices into columns[[k]] and columns[[k+1]]\n#'                   respectively. Repeat an index for many-to-many links.\n#' @param titles     optional character vector of column headings.\n#' @param rx, ry     ellipse half-width / half-height.\n#' @param dx, dy     horizontal spacing between columns / vertical\n#'                   spacing between ellipses within a column.\n#' @param wrap_width characters per line before wrapping label text.\n#' @param fill_colors  one fill colour per column; recycled/extended\n#'                   with a default pastel palette if too few given.\n#' @param curvature  passed to geom_curve (0 = straight lines).\n#'\n#' @return list(plot = ggplot object, nodes = node data frame,\n#'   edges = edge data frame used for the curves)\nconcept_map \u003c- function(columns, edges, titles = NULL,\n                         rx = 1.15, ry = 0.62, dx = 3.3, dy = 1.55,\n                         wrap_width = 16,\n                         fill_colors = c(\"#F6DCC7\", \"#CFE3F5\", \"#D9E8CE\", \"#E9D8F0\", \"#F5E3B0\"),\n                         edge_color = \"#888888\", edge_alpha = 0.7, curvature = 0.2) {\n  k \u003c- length(columns)\n  stopifnot(length(edges) == k - 1)\n  if (length(fill_colors) \u003c k) {\n    fill_colors \u003c- grDevices::colorRampPalette(fill_colors)(k)\n  }\n\n  # Validate edge indices up front, so a typo (e.g. an off-by-one, or a\n  # `from`/`to` swapped) fails with a message naming the column and the\n  # offending index -- rather than silently dropping the edge (as NA\n  # coordinates) later and only surfacing a generic ggplot warning.\n  for (ci in seq_len(k - 1)) {\n    e \u003c- edges[[ci]]\n    n_from \u003c- length(columns[[ci]])\n    n_to \u003c- length(columns[[ci + 1]])\n    bad_from \u003c- e$from[!(e$from %in% seq_len(n_from))]\n    bad_to \u003c- e$to[!(e$to %in% seq_len(n_to))]\n    if (length(bad_from) > 0) {\n      stop(sprintf(\n        \"edges[[%d]]$from has out-of-range value(s) %s: column %d ('%s') only has %d item(s), so valid indices are 1..%d.\",\n        ci, paste(unique(bad_from), collapse = \", \"), ci,\n        if (!is.null(titles)) titles[ci] else paste(\"column\", ci), n_from, n_from\n      ))\n    }\n    if (length(bad_to) > 0) {\n      stop(sprintf(\n        \"edges[[%d]]$to has out-of-range value(s) %s: column %d ('%s') only has %d item(s), so valid indices are 1..%d.\",\n        ci, paste(unique(bad_to), collapse = \", \"), ci + 1,\n        if (!is.null(titles)) titles[ci + 1] else paste(\"column\", ci + 1), n_to, n_to\n      ))\n    }\n  }\n\n  # node layout: column i at x = (i-1)*dx, items centred vertically\n  nodes \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(k), function(ci) {\n    labels \u003c- columns[[ci]]\n    m \u003c- length(labels)\n    data.frame(\n      col = ci, idx = seq_len(m),\n      x = (ci - 1) * dx,\n      y = (m - 1) / 2 * dy - (seq_len(m) - 1) * dy,\n      label = vapply(labels, wrap_label, character(1), width = wrap_width),\n      fill = fill_colors[ci],\n      stringsAsFactors = FALSE\n    )\n  }))\n  nodes$node_id \u003c- paste(nodes$col, nodes$idx)\n\n  node_xy \u003c- function(ci, i) {\n    row \u003c- nodes[nodes$col == ci & nodes$idx == i, ]\n    c(x = row$x, y = row$y)\n  }\n\n  # edge endpoints: right edge of source ellipse -> left edge of target\n  edge_df \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(k - 1), function(ci) {\n    e \u003c- edges[[ci]]\n    do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(nrow(e)), function(j) {\n      p1 \u003c- node_xy(ci, e$from[j])\n      p2 \u003c- node_xy(ci + 1, e$to[j])\n      data.frame(x = p1[\"x\"] + rx, y = p1[\"y\"], xend = p2[\"x\"] - rx, yend = p2[\"y\"])\n    }))\n  }))\n\n  # ellipse polygons\n  ellipse_df \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(nrow(nodes)), function(i) {\n    n \u003c- nodes[i, ]\n    e \u003c- ellipse_pts(n$x, n$y, rx, ry)\n    e$node_id \u003c- n$node_id\n    e$fill \u003c- n$fill\n    e\n  }))\n\n  title_df \u003c- NULL\n  if (!is.null(titles)) {\n    top_y \u003c- max(nodes$y) + ry + 0.6\n    title_df \u003c- data.frame(x = unique(nodes$x), y = top_y, label = titles)\n  }\n\n  plot \u003c- ggplot() +\n    geom_curve(data = edge_df, aes(x = x, y = y, xend = xend, yend = yend),\n               curvature = curvature, color = edge_color, alpha = edge_alpha,\n               linewidth = 0.6,\n               arrow = arrow(length = unit(0.12, \"cm\"), type = \"closed\")) +\n    geom_polygon(data = ellipse_df, aes(x, y, group = node_id, fill = fill),\n                 color = \"#555555\", linewidth = 0.4) +\n    scale_fill_identity() +\n    geom_text(data = nodes, aes(x, y, label = label), size = 3.2, lineheight = 0.9) +\n    coord_equal() +\n    theme_void()\n\n  if (!is.null(title_df)) {\n    plot \u003c- plot + geom_text(data = title_df, aes(x, y, label = label),\n                              fontface = \"bold\", size = 4.5)\n  }\n\n  list(plot = plot, nodes = nodes, edges = edge_df)\n}\n"},"elapsedMs":0}</script></tool-result></div>

rx, yend = p2[\"y\"])\n+      data.frame(x = p1[\"x\"] + p1[\"rx\"], y = p1[\"y\"],\n+                 xend = p2[\"x\"] - p2[\"rx\"], yend = p2[\"y\"])\n     }))\n   }))\n \n-  # ellipse polygons\n+  # ellipse polygons, each at its own node's rx/ry\n   ellipse_df \u003c- do.call(rbind, lapply(seq_len(nrow(nodes)), function(i) {\n     n \u003c- nodes[i, ]\n-    e \u003c- ellipse_pts(n$x, n$y, rx, ry)\n+    e \u003c- ellipse_pts(n$x, n$y, n$rx, n$ry)\n     e$node_id \u003c- n$node_id\n     e$fill \u003c- n$fill\n     e\n@@ -190,7 +195,7 @@\n \n   title_df \u003c- NULL\n   if (!is.null(titles)) {\n-    top_y \u003c- max(nodes$y) + ry + 0.6\n+    top_y \u003c- max(nodes$y + nodes$ry) + 0.6\n     title_df \u003c- data.frame(x = unique(nodes$x), y = top_y, label = titles)\n   }\n \n@@ -198,7 +203,7 @@\n     geom_curve(data = edge_df, aes(x = x, y = y, xend = xend, yend = yend),\n                curvature = curvature, color = edge_color, alpha = edge_alpha,\n                linewidth = 0.6,\n-               arrow = arrow(length = unit(0.12, \"cm\"), type = \"closed\")) +\n+               arrow = if (arrows) arrow(length = unit(0.12, \"cm\"), type = \"closed\") else NULL) +\n     geom_polygon(data = ellipse_df, aes(x, y, group = node_id, fill = fill),\n                  color = \"#555555\", linewidth = 0.4) +\n     scale_fill_identity() +\n","language":"diff"}}</script></tool-call></div>

All working as intended:

- **Default behavior unchanged** — regression check produced an identical render to before.
- **Plain-line variant** (`arrows = FALSE`) renders cleanly with no arrowheads, closer to McElreath's correspondence-not-causality framing.
- **Per-column sizing** works — the theories/stats columns (long sentences) got bigger ellipses and wider wrap, the process column (short labels, e.g. "Neural network connections change") stayed compact, and increasing `dx` to 4.2 kept everything clear of overlap.
- **Overlap warning** fires correctly when `dx` is too small for the requested per-column `rx` values, naming the two offending columns and their radii.
- **Length validation** rejects an `rx` vector of the wrong length with a clear message.

One thing to flag from the sized render: it now uses `dx = 4.2`, `rx` up to 1.6 — wider overall than the original. If you're targeting a specific slide width, you may want to nudge `dx`/`rx` down slightly rather than using my example values verbatim; I picked them just to demonstrate the feature.


<span class="suggestion">Tune `rx`/`ry`/`wrap_width`/`dx` together to match the actual slide dimensions in doing-being-science.qmd.</span>

inverted-T stand: narrow neck down to a wider foot\n    neck   = data.frame(x = x_offset + c(-neck_hw, neck_hw, neck_hw, -neck_hw),\n                         y = c(neck_top, neck_top, neck_bottom, neck_bottom)),\n    base   = data.frame(x = x_offset + c(-base_hw, base_hw, base_hw, -base_hw),\n                         y = c(base_top, base_top, base_bottom, base_bottom))\n  )\n  out \u003c- lapply(names(parts), function(nm) {\n    p \u003c- parts[[nm]]\n    data.frame(part = nm, x = ox + scale * p$x, y = oy + scale * p$y)\n  })\n  do.call(rbind, out)\n}\n\n#' One person + screen, as a single composite polygon set with a `role`\n#' column (\"person\" vs \"monitor\") so they can be filled differently.\nperson_at_computer \u003c- function(kind = \"A\", age = \"adult\", disabled = FALSE,\n                                scale = 1, ox = 0, oy = 0,\n                                person_color = \"#3B6EA5\",\n                                screen_color = \"#CFE8FF\",\n                                frame_color = \"#555555\") {\n  p \u003c- person_parts(kind, age, disabled, scale, ox, oy)\n  p$role \u003c- \"person\"\n  p$fill \u003c- person_color\n\n  m \u003c- monitor_parts(scale = scale, ox = ox, oy = oy)\n  m$role \u003c- \"monitor\"\n  m$fill \u003c- ifelse(m$part == \"screen\", screen_color, frame_color)\n\n  rbind(p, m)\n}\n\n# ---- main sequence function -----------------------------------------------\n\n#' Draw n copies of \"person at computer\".\n#'\n#' @param n        how many icons (1 = single participant, 2 = a pair,\n#'                 larger = a sample).\n#' @param scores   optional numeric vector of length n; if supplied, each\n#'                 icon gets an \"x = \u003cscore>\" label underneath (used for\n#'                 the n = 1 and n = 2 steps). Leave NULL for the sample\n#'                 grid step, where individual scores aren't shown.\n#' @param ncol     icons per row; default is n itself for n \u003c= 4, else\n#'                 a compact grid.\n#' @param comp_kind, comp_age, disabled_prop, seed: same composition\n#'                 controls as person_grid().\n#' @param mode     \"diversity\" (default; sampled skin-tone fill, as\n#'                 before) or \"value\" (continuous diverging fill driven\n#'                 by `value`, e.g. to colour icons by score/effect).\n#' @param skin_tones hex colours to sample from when mode = \"diversity\".\n#' @param value    numeric vector of length n; required when\n#'                 mode = \"value\". 0 = the null/reference point (see\n#'                 `diverging_colors()` in person-grid.R).\n#' @return list(plot = ggplot object, data = polygon data frame)\ntesting_icons \u003c- function(n, scores = NULL, ncol = NULL,\n                           comp_kind = c(A = 0.45, B = 0.45, N = 0.10),\n                           comp_age = c(adult = 1.0),\n                           disabled_prop = 0,\n                           mode = c(\"diversity\", \"value\"),\n                           skin_tones = c(\"#4A3728\", \"#7A5230\", \"#B07A4E\",\n                                          \"#D9A066\", \"#F0C892\", \"#F5E1C8\"),\n                           value = NULL,\n                           seed = 1, dx = 1.9, dy = 2.5) {\n  mode \u003c- match.arg(mode)\n  if (!is.null(scores)) stopifnot(length(scores) == n)\n  if (is.null(ncol)) ncol \u003c- if (n \u003c= 4) n else min(6, ceiling(sqrt(n)))\n  nrow_ \u003c- ceiling(n / ncol)\n\n  kinds \u003c- sample_counts(n, comp_kind, seed)\n  ages \u003c- sample_counts(n, comp_age, seed + 1)\n  disabled \u003c- sample_counts(n, c(yes = disabled_prop, no = 1 - disabled_prop), seed + 2) == \"yes\""},"elapsedMs":0}</script></tool-result></div>

<div><tool-call id="toolu_017FFii8qQcCk4VCP3tsybxE"><script type="application/json">{"version":2,"name":"edit","input":{"file_path":"testing-sequence.R","old_string":"  list(plot = plot, data = df)\n}","new_string":"  list(plot = plot, data = df)\n}\n\n#' A grid of icon-grids, one per \"study\" (facet_wrap(~st...[truncated, total 1693 chars]"},"caption":"testing-sequence.R","captionLink":"testing-sequence.R","inputPreview":{"type":"code","value":"@@ -172,3 +172,41 @@\n \n   list(plot = plot, data = df)\n }\n+\n+#' A grid of icon-grids, one per \"study\" (facet_wrap(~study)) -- the icon\n+#' analogue of sampling-distributions.R's plot_histogram_grid()/\n+#' plot_dotpile_grid(), for showing \"many studies, each with their own\n+#' independently-sampled group of people\".\n+#'\n+#' @param n_studies    number of study panels.\n+#' @param n_per_study  number of icons (people) per study panel.\n+#' @param ncol         panels per row (passed to facet_wrap).\n+#' @param seed         base seed; study s uses seed + s so each panel's\n+#'                     sample is independently drawn but reproducible.\n+#' @param ...          passed through to testing_icons() for each panel's\n+#'                     composition/colour controls (comp_kind, comp_age,\n+#'                     disabled_prop, mode, skin_tones, value, dx, dy).\n+#'                     `scores` isn't supported here (no labels, as for\n+#'                     the single-study sample grid).\n+#' @return list(plot = ggplot object, data = polygon data frame)\n+testing_icons_studies \u003c- function(n_studies, n_per_study, ncol = NULL,\n+                                   seed = 1, ...) {\n+  panels \u003c- vector(\"list\", n_studies)\n+  for (s in seq_len(n_studies)) {\n+    g \u003c- testing_icons(n = n_per_study, seed = seed + s, ...)\n+    g$data$study \u003c- paste(\"Study\", s)\n+    panels[[s]] \u003c- g$data\n+  }\n+  df \u003c- do.call(rbind, panels)\n+  df$poly_id \u003c- paste(df$study, df$poly_id)\n+\n+  plot \u003c- ggplot(df, aes(x, y, group = poly_id, fill = fill)) +\n+    geom_polygon() +\n+    scale_fill_identity() +\n+    coord_equal() +\n+    facet_wrap(~study, ncol = ncol) +\n+    theme_void() +\n+    theme(strip.text = element_text(face = \"bold\"))\n+\n+  list(plot = plot, data = df)\n+}\n","language":"diff"}}</script></tool-call></div>

<div><thinking id="thinking-1790523702174-sr4k4">
Slide 1's observed_score is coming out NA with seed=101, n=1 -- I need to dig into why, since calibrate_ability with mu=30 and reliability=0.8 normally works fine. Something specific to this seed must be triggering the NA.

Actually, I found it: with n=1, the code draws a single x value and calls scale(x), but scaling a single-element vector produces NA because sample standard deviation is undefined for n=1 (0/NA = NaN). Even though r_with_x defaults to 0, meaning that term should drop out, multiplying 0 by NaN still gives NaN in IEEE floating point rather than 0 -- that's the actual bug propagating NA all the way through.

This is a real, previously-undetected bug that's directly triggered by our single-participant use case, so it's blocking and needs fixing. The fix is to explicitly branch: use z directly when r_theta_x is 0, otherwise compute the weighted combination -- avoiding reliance on multiplication to zero out the problematic term.

For n=1, correlation is inherently undefined since there's only one point, so I should make the simulation gracefully skip correlation induction entirely in this case, forcing it to use z directly and warning the user if they requested a nonzero correlation that can't actually be honored with a single observation.

This same defensive check also protects against the more general "0 times NaN" issue whenever r_with_x is exactly 0, including edge cases like a constant x vector producing NaN/Inf from scaling. I want to implement this fix and retest, then also double-check that plot_dots correctly handles a single NA row being dropped with a warning.


</thinking><span data-thinking-duration-ms="13213"></span></div>

Ignored if\n#'                      `sd_true` is supplied.\n#' @param sd_true      alternate calibration target: the SD (0-n_items\n#'                      scale) of people's expected/\"true\" scores,\n#'                      calibrated directly instead of `reliability` --\n#'                      use this when a figure needs independent control\n#'                      of spread rather than of repeatability. <div><tool-result call-id="toolu_01RBjPJoPPzMsjP1ox1Dyskv"><script type="application/json">{"version":1,"status":"success","result":{"type":"text","value":"[File: 18.7KB, 403 lines, showing lines 237-266]\n237\t  if (!is.null(seed)) set.seed(seed)\n...[truncated, total 1318 chars]"},"resultPreview":{"type":"code","value":"[File: 18.7KB, 403 lines, showing lines 237-266]\n  if (!is.null(seed)) set.seed(seed)\n  if (!is.null(sd_true) && !missing(reliability)) {\n    warning(paste(\n      \"Both `reliability` and `sd_true` were supplied to\",\n      \"simulate_vocab_data(); they aren't independently settable\",\n      \"(fixing mu and sd_true determines reliability, and vice versa).\",\n      \"Using `sd_true` and ignoring `reliability`.\"\n    ))\n  }\n  cal \u003c- calibrate_ability(mu, reliability = reliability, sd_true = sd_true, n_items = n_items)\n  reliability \u003c- cal$moments$reliability\n\n  if (is.null(x)) x \u003c- rnorm(n, x_mean, x_sd)\n  xz \u003c- as.numeric(scale(x))\n\n  max_r \u003c- sqrt(reliability)\n  if (abs(r_with_x) > max_r) {\n    warning(sprintf(\n      paste(\"r_with_x = %.2f is not achievable at reliability = %.2f\",\n            \"(measurement error caps the observed-score correlation at\",\n            \"about %.2f); using %.2f instead.\"),\n      r_with_x, reliability, max_r, sign(r_with_x) * max_r\n    ))\n    r_with_x \u003c- sign(r_with_x) * max_r\n  }\n  r_theta_x \u003c- if (r_with_x == 0) 0 else r_with_x / max_r\n\n  z \u003c- rnorm(n)\n  theta_z \u003c- r_theta_x * xz + sqrt(1 - r_theta_x^2) * z\n  theta \u003c- cal$theta_mu + cal$theta_sigma * theta_z"},"elapsedMs":0}</script></tool-result></div>



<div><thinking id="thinking-1790523739857-zcalr">
Found the actual bug: scaling x produces NaN when n<2 or x is constant, and that NaN propagates even when it's multiplied by a zero coefficient. I need to restructure so the scaled x is only computed when actually needed, avoiding the NaN-times-zero trap entirely.

I'm adding validation for r_with_x against the max achievable correlation, handling n<2 by warning and zeroing r_with_x, skipping the scale() call entirely when r_theta_x is zero, and adding a defensive check for zero-variance x that falls back to ignoring r_with_x with a warning.


</thinking><span data-thinking-duration-ms="5534"></span></div>

Found a real bug: `scale(x)` returns `NaN` when `n < 2` (SD of a single point is undefined), and `0 * NaN = NaN` in R — so even with the default `r_with_x = 0`, a single-person simulation silently produced an `NA` score. This is exactly the slide-1 (`n = 1`) use case, so it needs fixing before I wire up the slides.

<div><tool-call id="toolu_018vur6vNa5qwLyp41zzLgpL"><script type="application/json">{"version":2,"name":"edit","input":{"file_path":"sampling-distributions.R","old_string":"  if (is.null(x)) x \u003c- rnorm(n, x_mean, x_sd)\n  xz \u003c- as.numeric(scale(x))\n\n  max_r \u003c- sqr...[truncated, total 641 chars]","new_string":"  if (is.null(x)) x \u003c- rnorm(n, x_mean, x_sd)\n\n  max_r \u003c- sqrt(reliability)\n  if (abs(r_wi...[truncated, total 1331 chars]"},"caption":"sampling-distributions.R","captionLink":"sampling-distributions.R","inputPreview":{"type":"code","value":"@@ -247,7 +247,6 @@\n   reliability \u003c- cal$moments$reliability\n \n   if (is.null(x)) x \u003c- rnorm(n, x_mean, x_sd)\n-  xz \u003c- as.numeric(scale(x))\n \n   max_r \u003c- sqrt(reliability)\n   if (abs(r_with_x) > max_r) {\n@@ -259,10 +258,28 @@\n     ))\n     r_with_x \u003c- sign(r_with_x) * max_r\n   }\n+  if (r_with_x != 0 && n \u003c 2) {\n+    warning(\"r_with_x is not meaningful for n \u003c 2 (correlation is undefined for a single observation); ignoring it.\")\n+    r_with_x \u003c- 0\n+  }\n   r_theta_x \u003c- if (r_with_x == 0) 0 else r_with_x / max_r\n \n+  # Only standardise x (and only use it) when it's actually needed: with\n+  # r_theta_x == 0, scale(x) may be NaN (e.g. n \u003c 2, or a constant x) and\n+  # 0 * NaN is NaN in R, not 0 -- multiplying it in unconditionally would\n+  # silently turn every theta (and hence every score) into NA.\n   z \u003c- rnorm(n)\n-  theta_z \u003c- r_theta_x * xz + sqrt(1 - r_theta_x^2) * z\n+  if (r_theta_x == 0) {\n+    theta_z \u003c- z\n+  } else {\n+    xz \u003c- as.numeric(scale(x))\n+    if (anyNA(xz)) {\n+      warning(\"x has zero variance (e.g. all identical values); r_with_x cannot be induced. Ignoring r_with_x.\")\n+      theta_z \u003c- z\n+    } else {\n+      theta_z \u003c- r_theta_x * xz + sqrt(1 - r_theta_x^2) * z\n+    }\n+  }\n   theta \u003c- cal$theta_mu + cal$theta_sigma * theta_z\n   p_correct \u003c- plogis(theta)\n \n","language":"diff"}}</script></tool-call></div>

**What's now in the deck**, under a run of five `## Given the logic, the challenges: ...` slides:

1. **One person, one score** — `testing_icons(n=1, scores=...)` beside `plot_dots()`, sharing one `simulate_vocab_data(n=1, seed=101)` draw so the icon's label and the point match.
2. **Two people, two scores** — same pairing at `n=2`.
3. **A sample of people** — a 20-icon grid (no score labels, matching the earlier design decision) beside a `plot_dotpile()` of 20 independently-simulated scores.
4. **Many studies, many samples** — a new `testing_icons_studies()` function (added to `testing-sequence.R`) faceting six independent 20-person icon grids into one figure.
5. **Distributions from repeated samples** — a new `plot_dotpile_grid()` function (added to `sampling-distributions.R`, mirroring the existing `plot_histogram_grid()`) faceting the corresponding six score distributions.

**One bug turned up and got fixed along the way:** `simulate_vocab_data(n = 1)` was silently returning `NA` for the score. `scale(x)` returns `NaN` when there's only one observation (SD is undefined), and `0 * NaN = NaN` in R — so even at the default `r_with_x = 0`, the NaN was leaking into every score. This exact case is slide 1's `n = 1`, so it would have shown a broken "x = NA" label. Fixed by skipping the `x`-standardization path entirely when `r_with_x == 0`, and by warning and ignoring `r_with_x` outright when `n < 2` (correlation is undefined for a single point). Verified it doesn't affect `r_with_x` behavior at larger `n`.

Both column chunks in slides 1–3 use `#| echo: false` to keep the slides clean (figure only, no visible R code) — let me know if you'd rather show the simulation code for teaching purposes.
